% sec_introduction.tex -- Introduction
\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}

The Cayley transform
\[
\iota(x)=\frac{1+\ii x}{1-\ii x}
\]
identifies the one-point compactification of \(\RR\) with \(\TT\), and pulls
normalized Haar measure back to the Cauchy law
\[
\omega(\dd x)=\frac{1}{\pi(1+x^{2})}\dd x=P_1(x)\dd x .
\]
This is the endpoint-normalized form of the classical Cauchy/wrapped-Cauchy
M{\"o}bius correspondence in circular statistics
\cite{KentTyler1988WrappedCauchy,McCullagh1992CauchyModels,
McCullagh1996CauchyMobius,
KatoJones2010MobiusCircle,KatoMcCullagh2020SphereCauchyMobius}.
This normalization makes the fixed-law Poisson comparison
\begin{equation}\label{eq:intro-main-functional}
D_{\mathrm{KL}}\bigl(P_t*\nu\,\|\,P_t(\cdot-\bar{\gamma})\bigr)
\end{equation}
the object whose large-\(t\) behaviour is studied in Cayley--Chebyshev
modes.  The input law \(\nu\) is fixed, \(\bar{\gamma}\) is its mean, and
\(t\to\infty\) is the Poisson scale.

The basic calculation is a quotient calculation.  Let
\[
X_c=\gamma-\bar{\gamma}
\]
be the centred translation variable.  With \(x=\bar{\gamma}+ty\), translation
by \(X_c\) is encoded by
\[
R_\varepsilon(y)=\frac{1+y^2}{1+(y-\varepsilon)^2},
\qquad \varepsilon=X_c/t .
\]
The Cayley angle \(y=\tan\theta\) turns the Taylor coefficients of
\(R_\varepsilon\) into finite trigonometric, equivalently Laurent, modes.
After the matched Cauchy profile has been divided out, the quotient expansion
is therefore reduced to finite mode algebra; the KL expansion is then the
ordinary Taylor expansion of \((1+u)\log(1+u)-u\) in
\(L^\infty\cap L^1(\omega)\).

\subsection*{Contribution}

The principal unconditional result is
Theorem~\ref{thm:sharp-leading-kl-variance-threshold}.  For every law with
finite mean and without a tail-class assumption, finite centred variance is
equivalent to bounded \(t^4D_{\rm KL}\); the limit is \(\mu_2^2/8\) in the
finite-variance case and \(+\infty\) otherwise.  The infinite-variance proof
pairs the quotient with the bounded second Cayley mode and therefore does not
use a formal Taylor expansion.

The other principal analytic result is the regularly varying boundary theorem,
Theorem~\ref{cor:main-body-conditional-missing-top-moment-tail}.  For \(N\ge3\),
a centred law with two-sided regularly varying tails of index \(p=2N-2\) and
divergent integrated slowly varying factor
\(\ell_L(t)=\int^tL(s)\dd s/s\) satisfies
\[
t^{2N}\left\{H(t)-\sum_{j=2}^{N-1}A_{2j}(\nu)t^{-2j}\right\}
\sim p(c_++c_-)\kappa_N\mu_2\ell_L(t),
\qquad
\kappa_N=(-1)^N(N-1)2^{-2N+2}.
\]
Equivalently, the residual normalized by the truncated boundary moment has
limit \(\kappa_N\mu_2\).  This is a class-conditional analytic statement,
without a density, symmetry, or monotonicity assumption; it is not an
unconditional higher-order converse.

The all-order coefficient algebra has a different logical status.  For every
\(m\ge3\), the reduced universal polynomial satisfies
\[
A_{2m}^{\rm form}
=\kappa_m\mathfrak m_2\mathfrak m_{2m-2}
+P_m(\mathfrak m_2,\ldots,\mathfrak m_{2m-3}),
\qquad \kappa_m=(-1)^m(m-1)2^{-2m+2}\ne0.
\]
At \(m=2\), one instead has \(A_4^{\rm form}=\mathfrak m_2^2/8\).
Thus \(\EE|X_c|^{2m-2}<\infty\) is sufficient to evaluate every monomial and
is the minimal absolute-moment assumption that guarantees such evaluation
uniformly over all centred laws.  Proposition~\ref{thm:sharp-moment-equivalence-hierarchy}
is only this algebraic polynomial-domain minimality statement.  In particular,
for \(m\ge3\) it is not an unconditional converse for the entropy remainder.

The second theorem is multidimensional and finite-moment.  For
\(L\ge d+1\), finite centred \(L\)-moment closes the large-translation
remainder in the matched radial Poisson quotient, gives an explicit
\(L^\infty\cap L^1(\Omega_d)\) jet, supplies the large-\(t\)
entropy-existence package, and hence gives the tensor KL expansion through
order \(L+2\).  The two-region expression is retained as the quantitative
remainder bound; in this range the precise statement is the equivalence
\(\mathcal R_{d,L}(t)=o(t^{-L})\) for the displayed functional
\eqref{eq:conditional-multidim-two-region-tail} and the finite
\(L\)-moment assumption.
The restriction \(L\ge d+1\) is sharp for this \(L^\infty\) quotient
topology, as shown by the spike construction below.  It is not claimed to be
necessary for the leading KL coefficient in a weaker \(L^2(\Omega_d)\) or
entropy topology; the optimal weaker threshold remains open.
The leading multidimensional covariance formula is deliberately read through
this analytic route.  Finite covariance alone determines the bounded quadratic
mode \(b_\Sigma\) and the algebraic number \(\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)\), but it
does not by itself make \(\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)\) the coefficient of
\eqref{eq:intro-main-functional}.  It becomes the leading KL coefficient only
after a quotient/existence bridge is in force, for example the
\((\mathrm{MPJ}_{d,L})+(\mathrm{EX}_{d,L})\) package supplied here by
finite \(L\)-moment with \(L\ge d+1\), or the separate second-order quotient
criterion stated in Section~\ref{sec:entropy-asymptotics}.

The third theorem is a domain-and-endpoint result for the standard
simultaneous-semigroup relative-entropy calculus.  For a symmetric jump
generator
\[
(L_Jf)(x)=\int\{f(y)-f(x)\}J(x,y)\,\dd y,
\qquad J(x,y)=J(y,x),
\]
and sufficiently regular positive solutions
\(\partial_t h_t=L_Jh_t\), \(\partial_t g_t=L_Jg_t\), set
\(u_t=h_t/g_t\).  The general generator identity is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:intro-general-two-solution-generator-identity}
-\frac{\dd}{\dd t}D_{\rm KL}(h_t\|g_t)
=\frac12\iint J(x,y)
\left[g_t(x)\Lambda(u_t(x),u_t(y))
+g_t(y)\Lambda(u_t(y),u_t(x))\right]\dd x\dd y.
\end{equation}
By exchanging \(x\) and \(y\) in the second term, the right side equals
\[
\iint J(x,y)g_t(x)\Lambda(u_t(x),u_t(y))\dd x\dd y.
\]
This is the logarithmic relative-\(\Phi\)-entropy identity for two solutions
of the same symmetric jump semigroup; its Bregman differentiation is not
claimed as new \cite{Chafai2004PhiEntropies,BakryCoulhonLedouxSaloffCoste1995}.

Under finite centred variance, Theorem~\ref{thm:main-C} verifies that the
moving matched Cauchy quotient lies in the domain of this identity on every
compact time window, with the generator normalized as the symmetric
\(1\)-stable Cauchy generator
\cite{Feller1971,Bertoin1996LevyProcesses,Sato1999LevyProcesses,
Applebaum2009LevyProcesses,Kwasnicki2017}.  The KL functional is then the
tail integral of the resulting nonlocal dissipation.  Here both the quotient
\[
u_t=\frac{h_t}{g_t}
\]
and the reference density \(g_t=P_t(\cdot-\bar\gamma)\) move with \(t\).
The logarithmic chain rule therefore first gives the two-pair identity
\begin{equation}\label{eq:intro-two-pair-chain-rule}
H'(t)=\langle \log u_t,\partial_t h_t\rangle
      -\langle u_t-1,\partial_t g_t\rangle,
\qquad H(t)=D_{\rm KL}(h_t\|g_t),
\end{equation}
not a single fixed-reference Dirichlet form.  Applying the Cauchy Green
formula separately to the pairs \((h_t,\log u_t)\) and \((g_t,u_t)\), and
using \(\int\partial_tg_t=0\), produces the algebraic difference
\[
(\rho_xa-\rho_yb)(\log a-\log b)-(\rho_x-\rho_y)(a-b)
=\rho_x\Lambda(a,b)+\rho_y\Lambda(b,a),
\]
where \(a=u_t(x)\), \(b=u_t(y)\), \(\rho_x=g_t(x)\),
\(\rho_y=g_t(y)\), and
\(\Lambda(a,b)=a\log(a/b)-a+b\).  This is the logarithmic case of the
Csisz{\'a}r--Morimoto--Ali--Silvey \(f\)-divergence/Bregman calculus
\cite{AliSilvey1966Divergence,Csiszar1967,Morimoto1963Markov,
Vajda1989TheoryDivergence,Bregman1967}.  The actual increment is to justify
these formal pairings on the noncompact Cauchy state space: finite variance
yields compact-window upper and lower quotient bounds, valid Green cutoffs,
and integrable domination of the Bregman kernel.  The separate endpoint proof
\(H(T)\to0\) then permits integration to infinity.

The coefficient-domain and boundary-layer increments are separate from
that dissipation identity.  Stable-semigroup de Bruijn and Hardy--Stein formulas identify an
entropy derivative or an integrated Bregman/Dirichlet form
\cite{DeBruijn1953,HirataNemotoYoshida2012RelativeEntropy,
Yoshida2017RelativeEntropyDiffusion,BanuelosBogdanLuks2016HardyStein,
Gutowski2023HardySteinPureJump,
BogdanGutowskiPietruskaPaluba2025PolarizedHardyStein,
Johnson2013StableDeBruijn,Toscani2016StableEntropy,
BobkovChistyakovGotze2013StableEntropy}.  They do not determine the large-time
coefficient residual for a fixed input law against a moving matched Cauchy
reference.  The analytic increments here are the unconditional bounded-mode
converse at the variance threshold and the signed,
truncated-moment-normalized residual within the stated regularly varying
class.  The all-order Cayley--Laurent calculation instead supplies a finite
polynomial-evaluation algorithm and its minimal universal absolute-moment
assumption.

\subsection*{Theorems}\label{subsec:main-results}

\begin{maintheorem}{Analytic variance threshold and coefficient-domain minimality}
\label{thm:main-A}
Let \(\nu\) be a probability law on \(\RR\) with finite mean
\(\bar\gamma\), and put \(X_c=\gamma-\bar\gamma\).  No variance or tail
assumption is imposed at this point.  Set
\[
H(t)=D_{\mathrm{KL}}\bigl(P_t*\nu\,\|\,P_t(\cdot-\bar\gamma)\bigr)
\]
with the extended nonnegative convention for relative entropy.  Then
\[
\EE X_c^2<\infty
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
\limsup_{t\to\infty}t^4H(t)<\infty .
\]
If the variance is finite, then \(H(t)<\infty\) for every \(t>0\) and
\[
\lim_{t\to\infty}t^4H(t)=\frac{(\EE X_c^2)^2}{8};
\]
if it is infinite, then
\[
\lim_{t\to\infty}t^4H(t)=+\infty
\]
in the extended sense.  No compact-support, exponential-tail, or
regular-variation hypothesis is used in this equivalence.

For the higher universal coefficients, the conclusion is algebraic rather
than an unconditional analytic converse.  For every \(m\ge3\), let
\(A_{2m}^{\rm form}\) be the reduced universal polynomial defined by the
constant-term rule below.  Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:main-A-moment-equivalence}
A_{2m}^{\rm form}
=\kappa_m\mathfrak m_2\mathfrak m_{2m-2}
+P_m(\mathfrak m_2,\ldots,\mathfrak m_{2m-3}),
\qquad
\kappa_m=(-1)^m(m-1)2^{-2m+2}\ne0,
\end{equation}
At \(m=2\), \(A_4^{\rm form}=\mathfrak m_2^2/8\).  Consequently, for every
\(m\ge2\),
\(\EE|X_c|^{2m-2}<\infty\) is sufficient for the canonical Lebesgue
evaluation of every monomial, and is minimal among absolute-moment
assumptions that guarantee evaluation uniformly over all centred probability
laws.  This statement uses the deliberately specified polynomial-evaluation
domain of Definition~\ref{def:canonical-coefficient-finiteness}; for
\(m\ge3\) it is not an unconditional converse for the entropy remainder.

The supporting coefficient expansion has the exact moment hypothesis as
follows.  Let \(N\ge2\), put \(L=2N-2\), and assume now that
\(\EE|X_c|^L<\infty\).  With
\[
\begin{aligned}
  \mu_n&=\int_{\RR} X_c^n\,\dd\nu(\gamma)\qquad(2\le n\le L),\\
  h_t&=P_t*\nu,\qquad
  g_t&=P_t(\cdot-\bar{\gamma}),
\end{aligned}
\]
one has
\[
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h_t\|g_t)
=\sum_{m=2}^{N}A_{2m}(\nu)t^{-2m}+o(t^{-2N}).
\]
The coefficients are part of the statement.  For \(2\le m\le N\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:main-A-intro-coefficients}
A_{2m}(\nu)=
\sum_{r=2}^{m}
\frac{(-1)^r}{r(r-1)}
\sum_{\substack{n_1+\cdots+n_r=2m\\ n_j\ge2}}
\mu_{n_1}\cdots\mu_{n_r}
\,[z^0]\prod_{j=1}^r Q_{n_j}(z),
\end{equation}
where \([z^0]\) denotes constant Laurent coefficient and the
Cayley--Chebyshev Laurent representative is fixed by the convention
\begin{equation}\label{eq:main-A-intro-Qn}
Q_n(z)=2^{-n}(-\ii)^n
\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\binom{n-1}{\ell-1}
\bigl(z^\ell+(-1)^nz^{-\ell}\bigr)
\qquad(n\ge1).
\end{equation}
Thus \(A_{2m}(\nu)\) is a universal polynomial in
\(\mu_2,\ldots,\mu_{2m-2}\); the proof that
\eqref{eq:main-A-intro-coefficients} is the entropy coefficient is supplied
by Theorem~\ref{thm:poisson-kl-even-orders}.  All retained odd coefficients
vanish, and the leading coefficient is
\[
A_4(\nu)=\frac{\mu_2^2}{8}.
\]
\end{maintheorem}

\begin{maintheorem}{Finite-moment multidimensional Poisson entropy jet}
\label{thm:main-B}
Let \(d\ge1\), let \(L\in\NN\) satisfy \(L\ge d+1\), and let \(\nu\) be a
probability measure on \(\RR^d\) with mean \(\bar\gamma\).  Put
\[
X_c=\gamma-\bar\gamma .
\]
Assume \(\EE |X_c|^L<\infty\).  Then the centred moments
\[
\mu_\alpha=\EE[X_c^\alpha]\qquad(|\alpha|\le L)
\]
are finite.  The explicit two-region condition used in the quotient proof is
only the following one:
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal R_{d,L}(t)
&:=t^{-(L+1)}\EE\!\left[
|X_c|^{L+1}{\bf1}_{\{|X_c|\le t\}}\right] \\
&\quad{}+ t^{-L}\EE\!\left[
|X_c|^L{\bf1}_{\{|X_c|>t\}}\right],
\end{aligned}
\]
one has, in the stated range \(L\ge d+1\),
\[
\mathcal R_{d,L}(t)=o(t^{-L})
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
\EE |X_c|^L<\infty .
\]
No broader equivalence between arbitrary two-region estimates and moment
hypotheses is asserted.  The restriction \(L\ge d+1\) is sharp for this
\(L^\infty\) quotient topology: for every integer \(1\le L<d+1\), the spike
construction following Theorem~\ref{thm:second-order-quotient-kl-coefficient}
gives a centred law with finite \(L\)th absolute moment whose quotient does
not converge uniformly to one.  This does not establish necessity for a
leading KL coefficient under a weaker \(L^2(\Omega_d)\) or entropy topology.
With
\[
h_t=P_t^{(d)}*\nu,\qquad
g_t=P_t^{(d)}(\cdot-\bar\gamma),
\]
one has, for all sufficiently large \(t\),
\[
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h_t\|g_t)
=\sum_{m=4}^{L+2}\mathcal A_m^{(d)}(\nu)t^{-m}
+o(t^{-(L+2)}),
\]
where, for \(4\le m\le L+2\),
\[
\mathcal A_m^{(d)}(\nu)=
\sum_{r=2}^{\lfloor m/2\rfloor}
\frac{(-1)^r}{r(r-1)}
\sum_{\substack{\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_r\in\NN_0^d\\
|\alpha_1|+\cdots+|\alpha_r|=m\\
2\le|\alpha_j|\le L\ \mathrm{for}\ 1\le j\le r}}
\mu_{\alpha_1}\cdots\mu_{\alpha_r}
\int_{\RR^d}\prod_{j=1}^r
U_{\alpha_j}^{(d)}(y)\,\Omega_d(\dd y).
\]
Here \(U_\alpha^{(d)}\) denotes the radial Poisson Taylor mode and
\[
\Omega_d(\dd y)
=\frac{\Gamma((d+1)/2)}{\pi^{(d+1)/2}}
(1+|y|^2)^{-(d+1)/2}\,\dd y
\]
is the \(t=1\) \(d\)-dimensional Poisson law.  The displayed formula is a
finite universal tensor polynomial in the centred moments of orders
\(2,\ldots,m-2\), and all retained odd coefficients vanish.
For the leading retained coefficient, let
\[
\Sigma=\EE[X_cX_c^{\mathsf T}],\qquad
\Sigma_0=\Sigma-\frac{\operatorname{tr}\Sigma}{d}I_d .
\]
Then
\[
\mathcal A_4^{(d)}(\nu)=\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)
=c_d^{\rm iso}(\operatorname{tr}\Sigma)^2
+c_d^{\rm tr}\|\Sigma_0\|_{\rm HS}^2,
\]
with
\[
c_d^{\rm iso}=
\frac{3(d+1)(7d+9)}
{4d(d+3)(d+5)(d+7)},
\qquad
c_d^{\rm tr}=
\frac{3(d+1)(d+3)}
{4(d+5)(d+7)} .
\]
Equivalently,
\[
\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)
=\frac12\int_{\RR^d}b_\Sigma(y)^2\,\Omega_d(\dd y),
\quad
b_\Sigma(y)=
\frac{d+1}{2}\left(
-\frac{\operatorname{tr}\Sigma}{1+|y|^2}
+\frac{(d+3)y^{\mathsf T}\Sigma y}{(1+|y|^2)^2}
\right).
\]
\end{maintheorem}

\begin{maintheorem}{Domain and endpoint closure for Cauchy relative entropy}
\label{thm:main-C}
Let \(\nu\) be a probability measure on \(\RR\) with mean \(\bar{\gamma}\) and
finite centred second moment.  Put
\[
\begin{aligned}
h_t&=P_t*\nu,\\
g_t&=P_t(\cdot-\bar{\gamma}),\\
u_t&=h_t/g_t .
\end{aligned}
\]
Define the Bregman kernel by
\[
\Lambda(\alpha,\beta)=\alpha\log\frac{\alpha}{\beta}-\alpha+\beta,
\]
and define the nonlocal Bregman dissipation by the nonnegative Lebesgue
integral
\[
\mathcal I_\nu(t)
=\frac1\pi\iint_{\RR^2}
\frac{g_t(x)\Lambda(u_t(x),u_t(y))}{(x-y)^2}\dd x\dd y ,
\]
with the diagonal ignored.  For the Cauchy jump kernel
\(J(x,y)=\{\pi(x-y)^2\}^{-1}\), this is exactly the right side of the
general two-solution identity
\eqref{eq:intro-general-two-solution-generator-identity}.  The assertion is
that the matched quotient belongs to the required domain: \(\mathcal I_\nu\)
is measurable and locally integrable on \((0,\infty)\).  More precisely, on every compact window
\([a,b]\Subset(0,\infty)\), the quotient \(u_t\) satisfies the bounded
Lipschitz estimates, Green-cutoff convergence, and integrable Bregman-kernel
domination recorded in
Proposition~\ref{prop:main-C-domain-differentiation-package}.  These bounds
justify the general generator identity, while the separate endpoint result
\(D_{\rm KL}(h_T\|g_T)\to0\) justifies passage from a finite time window to
infinity.  Consequently, for every \(t>0\),
\[
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h_t\|g_t)=\int_t^\infty \mathcal I_\nu(s)\,\dd s .
\]
\end{maintheorem}

\subsection*{Comparison by mechanism}
\label{subsec:intro-recent-jfa-comparison}

Classical moment decompositions expand a function or convolution solution
into masses and derivatives of a fundamental kernel; see
Duoandikoetxea--Zuazua \cite{DuoandikoetxeaZuazua1992} and the subsequent
large-time diffusion literature
\cite{EscobedoZuazua1991,IshigeKawakamiMichihisa2017,
IshigeKawakamiMichihisa2022RefinedFractional}.  The density-level template is
\(P_t*\nu=\sum_{|\alpha|\le L}(-1)^{|\alpha|}\mu_\alpha
\partial^\alpha P_t/\alpha!+\text{remainder}\).  The increment in
Theorem~\ref{thm:main-B} is the uniform matched quotient estimate, including
large translations, followed by a justified KL transfer; it is not the
moment decomposition by itself.

Relative \(\Phi\)-entropy and pure-jump semigroup calculus already provide
the formal two-solution identity
\eqref{eq:intro-general-two-solution-generator-identity}; see Chafa{\"\i}
\cite{Chafai2004PhiEntropies} and, for early nonlinear/nonlocal energy forms,
Bakry--Coulhon--Ledoux--Saloff-Coste
\cite{BakryCoulhonLedouxSaloffCoste1995}.  Theorem~\ref{thm:main-C} therefore
claims only the finite-variance domain verification for the noncompact Cauchy
kernel and the endpoint passage that converts the window identity into a
tail integral.

At formula level, the polarized Hardy--Stein identity
\cite{BogdanGutowskiPietruskaPaluba2025PolarizedHardyStein} states, for its
symmetric Markov semigroup and \(p\ge2\),
\[
\int f g^{\langle p-1\rangle}\dd x
=\int_0^\infty\!\iint
\mathcal J_p(P_s\Phi(x),P_s\Phi(y))J(x,y)\dd x\dd y\dd s,
\qquad \Phi=(f,g),
\]
where, for \(w,z\in\RR^2\),
\[
\mathcal J_p(w,z)=z_1z_2^{\langle p-1\rangle}
-w_1w_2^{\langle p-1\rangle}-w_2^{\langle p-1\rangle}(z_1-w_1)
-(p-1)w_1|w_2|^{p-2}(z_2-w_2).
\]
This is a fixed power pairing.  It does not specialize directly to
\[
\int h_t\log(h_t/g_t)\dd x,
\]
nor does its \(p\ge2\) statement supply the logarithmic endpoint.  More
importantly for the present theorem,
it does not by specialization verify the compact-window bounds and noncompact
Green pairings for \(h_t/g_t\), or prove \(H(T)\to0\).  Those are precisely
the conclusions isolated in Theorem~\ref{thm:main-C}.  A broader comparison
by mechanism is given in Appendix~\ref{app:related-work-scope}.

\subsection*{Hypothesis ledger}
\label{subsec:intro-hypothesis-ledger}

The theorem statements above are intended to be read with the following
minimal hypothesis map.

\begin{center}
{\small
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.15}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.20\linewidth}|%
>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.30\linewidth}|%
>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X}
\textbf{result}
& \textbf{analytic hypothesis}
& \textbf{what it supplies}\\
\hline
Theorem~\ref{thm:main-A}, polynomial-domain minimality
& finite mean; the coefficient domain is the canonical polynomial-evaluation
domain of Definition~\ref{def:canonical-coefficient-finiteness}.
& finite \((2m-2)\)nd absolute moment is the minimal assumption guaranteeing
universal polynomial evaluation; no higher-order analytic converse is
asserted.\\
\hline
Theorem~\ref{thm:main-A}, supporting coefficient expansion
& \(N\ge2\), finite centred \((2N-2)\)nd absolute moment; no stronger tail
class.
& Laurent-mode coefficient expansion through \(t^{-2N}\).\\
\hline
Theorem~\ref{thm:main-A}, threshold
& finite mean for the extended statement; finite variance exactly for the
finite \(t^4\) coefficient.
& finite variance gives \(A_4=(\EE X_c^2)^2/8\); infinite variance gives
\(t^4H(t)\to+\infty\).\\
\hline
Theorem~\ref{cor:main-body-conditional-missing-top-moment-tail}
& \(N\ge3\); centred law with two-sided tails
\(c_\pm t^{-(2N-2)}L(t)\); measurable eventually positive slowly varying
\(L\); \(\int^tL(s)\dd s/s\to\infty\).
& the exact \(t^{-2N}M_{2N-2}(t)\) entropy residual, equivalently the
explicit integrated slowly varying normalization and limiting constant.\\
\hline
Theorem~\ref{thm:main-B}
& \(d\ge1\), \(L\ge d+1\), finite centred \(L\)th absolute moment.
& the displayed functional \(\mathcal R_{d,L}\) satisfies
\(o(t^{-L})\), hence the quotient jet and the large-\(t\) KL representation;
the KL tensor expansion and parity cancellation then follow.\\
\hline
Theorem~\ref{thm:main-C}
& finite centred second moment in dimension one.
& compact-window bounded quotient, \(L^1\)-absolute continuity, Cauchy Green
cutoff convergence, local integrability of \(\mathcal I_\nu\), and the tail
identity after the separate endpoint \(H(T)\to0\).\\
\end{tabularx}
}
\end{center}

\subsection*{Convention and organization}

% material to ancillary files; preserve the proved statements during that pass.

The paper uses the extended nonnegative Kullback--Leibler functional of
\eqref{eq:extended-kl-definition}.

Section~\ref{sec:preliminaries} fixes notation.  Sections~\ref{sec:cayley-gate}
and~\ref{sec:haar-pullback} record the Cayley and Haar-pullback
normalizations, including the notation table used in the entropy arguments.
Section~\ref{sec:entropy-asymptotics} proves the coefficient hierarchy, the
variance threshold, and the multidimensional quotient criteria.  It begins
with the detailed proof map, so the introduction need not carry the internal
hypothesis diagram.  Section~\ref{sec:doob-phi-entropy} proves the
compact-window Bregman domain bounds and the Cauchy/KL tail identity.  The
appendices contain the related-work table, normalization details, auxiliary
coefficient certificates, and the proof of the regularly varying
missing-moment boundary layer.

% sec_preliminaries.tex -- Preliminaries
\section{Cayley--Haar preliminaries}%
\label{sec:preliminaries}

We fix the notation and normalizations used throughout the paper.  The
same section also records the only circle-theoretic input needed later:
the Cayley chart identifies normalized Haar measure on~$\TT$ with the
Cauchy weight on~$\RR$.

Write
\[
\TT:=\{z\in\CC:\abs{z}=1\},\qquad
\DD:=\{z\in\CC:\abs{z}<1\},\qquad
\UU:=\Bigl(-\tfrac12,\tfrac12\Bigr).
\]
The normalized Haar measure on~$\TT$ is
\[
\dd m_{\TT}(e^{\ii\theta})=\frac{1}{2\pi}\dd\theta,
\qquad
\theta\in(-\pi,\pi].
\]
We write
\[
\overline\RR:=\RR\cup\{\infty\}
\]
for the one-point compactification of~$\RR$.  For $f\in L^{1}(\RR)$ we
use the Fourier transform
\[
\widehat f(\xi):=\int_{\RR}f(x)e^{-\ii x\xi}\dd x,
\qquad \xi\in\RR,
\]
so that $\widehat{f*g}=\widehat f\,\widehat g$ whenever both sides are
defined.
For $f\in L^{1}(\RR)\cap L^{2}(\RR)$ we also use the inversion and
Plancherel formulas
\[
f(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\RR}\widehat f(\xi)e^{\ii x\xi}\dd\xi,
\qquad
\|f\|_{L^2(\RR)}^{2}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\|\widehat f\|_{L^2(\RR)}^{2}
\]
\cite[Chapter~I]{Stein1971}.

If $X$ is a real-valued random variable with density $f_X$ with respect
to Lebesgue measure, its differential entropy is
\[
h_{\mathrm{diff}}(X):=-\int_{\RR}f_X(x)\log f_X(x)\dd x,
\]
whenever the integral is well defined.  If $f$ and $g$ are probability
density functions on~$\RR$, the Kullback--Leibler divergence
\cite{KullbackLeibler1951,CoverThomas2006} is used throughout as an
extended nonnegative functional.  Precisely, if \(f\not\ll g\), then
\[
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(f\|g):=+\infty .
\]
If \(f\ll g\), write \(u=f/g\) on \(\{g>0\}\) and set \(u=1\) on
\(\{g=0\}\).  Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:extended-kl-definition}
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(f\|g)
:=\int_{\RR}\{u(x)\log u(x)-u(x)+1\}\,g(x)\dd x
\in[0,\infty],
\end{equation}
with the convention \(0\log0=0\).  Equivalently, when
\(f\log(f/g)\) is integrable in the ordinary sense, this equals
\[
\int_{\RR}f(x)\log\frac{f(x)}{g(x)}\dd x .
\]
Thus the symbol \(D_{\mathrm{KL}}\) never presupposes finiteness.  In the
applications below \(g\) is a positive Poisson density, so absolute
continuity is automatic; under finite centred second moment
Lemma~\ref{lem:kl-bounded-test-and-poisson-quotient} gives
\(D_{\mathrm{KL}}(P_t*\nu\|P_t(\cdot-\bar{\gamma}))<\infty\) for every
\(t>0\).  Without that finite-variance hypothesis, all KL statements are
read in the extended interval \([0,\infty]\).

\begin{lemma}[KL bounded-test and Poisson quotient estimates]%
\label{lem:kl-bounded-test-and-poisson-quotient}
\label{lem:bounded-test-pinsker}
\label{lem:finite-time-poisson-kl-finite}
\label{lem:sharp-threshold-bounded-test-pinsker}
The following elementary estimates will be used repeatedly.

\begin{enumerate}
\item Let \(f\) and \(g\) be probability densities on~\(\RR\), and interpret
\(D_{\mathrm{KL}}(f\|g)\) by \eqref{eq:extended-kl-definition}.  If
\(\varphi\in L^\infty(\RR)\), then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:prelim-bounded-test-pinsker}
\left|\int_{\RR}\varphi(x)\{f(x)-g(x)\}\dd x\right|^2
\le 2\|\varphi\|_\infty^2D_{\mathrm{KL}}(f\|g).
\end{equation}
The inequality is an extended-valued statement: if the right-hand side is
\(+\infty\), it is read as a trivial bound.

\item Let \(\nu\) be a probability measure on~\(\RR\) with mean
\(\bar{\gamma}\) and finite centred second moment.  Define
\[
P_t(x):=\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{t}{t^2+x^2},\qquad
h_t=P_t*\nu,\qquad g_t=P_t(\cdot-\bar{\gamma}),\qquad u_t=h_t/g_t .
\]
For every compact interval \(0<a<b<\infty\), the quotient \(u_t\) is bounded
above and below by positive constants uniformly for
\((t,x)\in[a,b]\times\RR\).  More precisely, with
\(\mu_2=\int(\gamma-\bar\gamma)^2\dd\nu(\gamma)\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:prelim-poisson-quotient-two-sided-bound}
\frac{1}{2(1+\mu_2/a^2)}
\le u_t(x)\le 2+\frac{2\mu_2}{a^2}
\qquad(t\in[a,b],\ x\in\RR).
\end{equation}

\item Under the hypotheses of the preceding item,
\[
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h_t\|g_t)<\infty\qquad(t>0).
\]
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For the bounded-test estimate, if \(D_{\mathrm{KL}}(f\|g)=+\infty\) there is
nothing to prove.  Otherwise \(f\ll g\), and the usual
Csisz{\'a}r--Pinsker inequality for natural logarithms gives
\[
\|f-g\|_{L^1(\RR)}^2\le 2D_{\mathrm{KL}}(f\|g).
\]
Since \(\varphi\) is bounded,
\[
\left|\int_{\RR}\varphi(f-g)\dd x\right|
\le \|\varphi\|_\infty\|f-g\|_{L^1(\RR)} ,
\]
which proves \eqref{eq:prelim-bounded-test-pinsker}.

Let \(\nu\) be a probability measure on~\(\RR\) with mean \(\bar{\gamma}\) and
finite centred second moment.  Define
\[
P_t(x):=\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{t}{t^2+x^2},\qquad
h_t=P_t*\nu,\qquad g_t=P_t(\cdot-\bar{\gamma}),\qquad u_t=h_t/g_t .
\]
Write \(X_c=\gamma-\bar{\gamma}\), \(z=(x-\bar{\gamma})/t\), and
\[
R_\varepsilon(z):=\frac{1+z^2}{1+(z-\varepsilon)^2}.
\]
Then
\[
u_t(x)=\int_{\RR}R_{X_c/t}(z)\,\dd\nu(\gamma).
\]
The elementary inequalities
\[
R_\varepsilon(z)\le 2(1+\varepsilon^2),
\qquad
R_\varepsilon(z)\ge \frac{1}{2(1+\varepsilon^2)}
\]
give, with \(\mu_2=\EE X_c^2\),
\[
\frac{1}{2(1+\mu_2/a^2)}
\le u_t(x)\le 2+\frac{2\mu_2}{a^2}
\qquad (t\in[a,b],\ x\in\RR),
\]
where the lower bound uses Jensen's inequality for
\(s\mapsto(1+s)^{-1}\).  Fix \(t>0\) and choose \(a<t<b\).  If
\(m\le u_t\le M\), then
\[
0\le D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h_t\|g_t)
=\int_{\RR}u_t(x)\log u_t(x)\,g_t(x)\dd x
\le M\max\{|\log m|,|\log M|\}<\infty .
\]
\end{proof}
We shall repeatedly use the elementary identity
\[
\int_{\RR}(f-g)\dd x=0
\]
whenever $f$ and $g$ are probability densities.

..\2026_zeckendorf_stable_arithmetic_fibonacci_congruence_online\main.tex:54:We study arithmetic induced by Zeckendorf representations and show that the relevant algebraic structure is forced by local Fibonacci rewriting rather than inserted a priori. Starting from the free commutative monoid of scale-counting configurations, we quotient by the Fibonacci relations and prove that the resulting monoid is canonically isomorphic to \(\NN\), with the Zeckendorf language furnishing a unique computable normal representative in each class. At finite resolution \(m\), the stable value map \(V_m:X_m\to \ZZ/(F_{m+2}\ZZ)\) transports addition and multiplication to a commutative ring structure on \(X_m\), yielding field phases when \(F_{m+2}\) is prime and Chinese-remainder decompositions when it is composite. We then connect this algebraic picture with online Zeckendorf addition, multiplicative generation by iteration, and a Fibonacci-adic inverse-limit ring obtained from the divisibility chain \(F_{2^k}\mid F_{2^{k+1}}\).
..\2026_zeckendorf_stable_arithmetic_fibonacci_congruence_online\main.tex:71:The present paper develops that algebraic viewpoint. Our starting point is not the semiring \((\NN,+,\times)\), but the free commutative monoid of scale-counting configurations together with the local Fibonacci relations. We show that quotienting by those relations produces \(\NN\) canonically, while the Zeckendorf language provides a computable transversal of the quotient. At finite resolution \(m\), the stable value map \(V_m\) identifies admissible words of length \(m\) with the residue ring \(\ZZ/(F_{m+2}\ZZ)\), so that addition and multiplication on stable addresses are obtained by transport of structure. This yields a concrete commutative ring on \(X_m\), field phases when \(F_{m+2}\) is prime, and canonical Chinese-remainder splittings when \(F_{m+2}\) is composite.

..\2026_zeckendorf_stable_arithmetic_fibonacci_congruence_online\main.tex:219:The quotient description of \(\NN\) immediately suggests a finite-resolution version: instead of allowing arbitrary support, we work inside the admissible window \(X_m\) and ask which arithmetic is visible at depth \(m\). ..\2026_zeckendorf_stable_arithmetic_fibonacci_congruence_online\main.tex:273:The ``field phase'' phenomenon is especially striking. Whenever \(F_{m+2}\) is prime, the stable arithmetic on \(X_m\) is literally the finite field \(\mathbb{F}_{F_{m+2}}\); when \(F_{m+2}\) is composite, the same address space decomposes canonically through the Chinese remainder theorem. This suggests a resolution-dependent supply of sparse coordinate systems for modular arithmetic. Any cryptographic application, however, would require much more than the present structural observations: one would need effective control of the distribution of Fibonacci primes, the behavior of unit groups, leakage under normalization algorithms, and implementation costs in concrete architectures \cite{Wall1960,Knuth1988FibonacciMultiplication,Koshy2018}.

..\submitted_2026_finite_window_rigidity_fibonacci_numeration_fq\main.tex:57:Equivalently, for $m\ge 3$ the residue vector modulo $F_{m+2}$ of the
..\submitted_2026_sharp_three_window_threshold_fibonacci_conjugacy_nonlinearity\sections_jpa\01_introduction.tex:9:the map that sends a binary word to the unique Fibonacci-admissible word with the same valuation modulo $F_{m+2}$. ..\submitted_2026_sharp_three_window_threshold_fibonacci_conjugacy_nonlinearity\sections_jpa\00_abstract.tex:6:which sends a binary word to the unique admissible Fibonacci word with the same valuation modulo $F_{m+2}$. The local map is many-to-one on isolated windows, but the relevant question is whether this ambiguity survives under sliding overlap. We prove a sharp threshold at $m=3$. For every $m\ge 3$, the overlapping stabilized-window map is injective, admits an explicit finite-memory inverse, and identifies its image with a subshift of finite type topologically conjugate to the full two-shift. At $m=2$, the failure of injectivity is completely concentrated on a unique branch orbit; this yields exact formulas for the conditional entropy and Kullback--Leibler defect and implies mod-zero invertibility off the branch set. On the arithmetic side, stabilization fibers admit an exact residue characterization and a discrete Fourier formula, and their second moment satisfies a linear recurrence. On path space, the conjugacy transports invariant measures, entropy, continuous potentials, pressure, and equilibrium states exactly. In the Sturmian benchmark this also yields deterministic discrepancy bounds for stabilized block laws and for the recovery of finite-range observables. The cut-and-project input is used only to fix the source law and admissible grammar; the theorem-level core is symbolic and arithmetic.

..\2026_sharp_three_window_threshold_fibonacci_conjugacy_dcds\submission_package\sections\00_abstract.tex:2:We study the finite-window Fibonacci normalization which sends a binary word of length $m$ to the unique golden-mean admissible word with the same Fibonacci valuation modulo $F_{m+2}$. The map is usually many-to-one on a single window, but its sliding overlap extension has a sharp threshold. We prove that for every $m\ge3$ it is a finite-memory conjugacy from the full two-shift onto a subshift of finite type, with an explicit decoder. For $m=2$ the only nontrivial path-space fiber is the pair of constant sequences; this yields exact conditional-entropy and Kullback--Leibler defect formulas and a mod-zero inverse off the branch. The proof combines a bi-resolving overlap presentation with a residue decoder, so the degree-one conclusion is an arithmetic fact rather than a formal consequence of resolving theory. Within the constant-type metallic model of Definition~\ref{def:metallic-window-data}, we also show that the golden case is exceptional: every integer parameter $A\ge2$ is already invertible at window length two. Above threshold the overlap presentation is identified with the right Fischer cover. Further residue, Fourier, second-moment and Sturmian discrepancy estimates record the associated finite-window statistics.

..\submitted_2026_resolution_folding_core_symbolic_dynamics_jnt\main.tex:57:Equivalently, for $m\ge 3$ the residue vector modulo $F_{m+2}$ of the
..\submitted_2026_resolution_folding_core_symbolic_dynamics_jnt\cover_letter.txt:5:The paper investigates a natural arithmetic question: given overlapping windows of fixed length m sliding along an arbitrary binary string, when do the local Zeckendorf normal forms--equivalently, the local Fibonacci weighted sums modulo F_{m+2}--determine the original string uniquely? We prove that m >= 3 is the sharp threshold. This local congruence rigidity yields several exact consequences: a bijective block count |L_n(Y_m)| = 2^{n+m-1}, explicit formulas for the distribution of random local Fibonacci residues under Bernoulli input, an exact m-step Markov law for the residue process, and optimal bit-recovery rules from residue windows. The proofs combine elementary properties of Zeckendorf representations with techniques from symbolic dynamics.

..\submitted_2026_shell_geometry_detector_thermality_kms_grg\paper\sections\A1_companion_input.tex:29:By the Zeckendorf representation theorem \cite{LindMarcus1995}, every non-negative integer $N$ admits a unique representation as a sum of non-consecutive Fibonacci numbers. If $0\le N<F_{m+2}$, then the largest Fibonacci weight that can appear is at most $F_{m+1}$, so the representation has length at most $m$ and defines a unique element of $X_m$. Conversely, every $x\in X_m$ satisfies $\mathrm{Val}_m(x)\le F_{m+1}+F_{m-1}+\cdots<F_{m+2}$ by the standard Fibonacci summation identity. Uniqueness of the Zeckendorf representation implies injectivity; surjectivity follows because every integer in $\{0,\dots,F_{m+2}-1\}$ has a Zeckendorf expansion of length at most $m$.

..\2026_sharp_three_window_threshold_fibonacci_conjugacy_dcds\sections\02_stabilization.tex:26:\Val_m|_{X_m}:X_m\longrightarrow \{0,1,\dots,F_{m+2}-1\}
..\2026_sharp_three_window_threshold_fibonacci_conjugacy_dcds\sections\02_stabilization.tex:36:\cite{Zeckendorf1972,Lothaire2002}. Referee report
1. Overall assessment
Major revision
The finite identities in Sections 3–6 appear mathematically sound; I independently checked the fiber identities, extremal values, maximizing residues, and quadratic recurrence for small and moderate windows, without finding counterexamples.
However, the principal new result—Theorem 4.5—is not yet proved at publication standard. Its argument requires the uniform bound

$$N_j(k)\le 2\Psi(k)\qquad\text{for every }j\ge0,$$

whereas Weinstein’s published theorem establishes eventual equality only once the layer index exceeds  $2k$ . The manuscript proposes a plausible strengthening but presently supplies only an informal description of the orbit geometry. Since Theorem 4.5, the endpoint  $t=-\sigma _0$ , and Proposition 4.6 all depend on that strengthening, this is a blocker.
The paper should not be rejected outright: the missing argument appears repairable from Weinstein’s explicit formulas. Nevertheless, most other results are elementary transfers or reformulations of known partition results, so acceptance depends heavily on making the freezing theorem completely rigorous and moderating its current “threshold” interpretation.

6. Concrete fixes for all BLOCKER and MEDIUM issues
I-01: rigorous uniform layer-count proof
Let  $f_j=F_{j+1}$  be Weinstein’s convention and define

$$L(n):=\max Z(n),$$

the largest index in the Zeckendorf partition of  $n$ . For  $R(n)>1$ , Weinstein’s Lemma 2.19 gives

$$n\in [f_j-1,f_{j+1}-1)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad L(n)=j,$$

because the exceptional endpoints have partition value  $1$ .
For  $k>2$ , every solution lies uniquely in an orbit

$$[a]\tau^\varepsilon\sigma^\delta(g),
\qquad
g\in G(k),\quad a\ge0,\quad \varepsilon,\delta\in\{0,1\}.$$

The authors must derive from Weinstein’s formulas (17)–(19) the explicit index relations

$$L([a]g)=L(g)+2a,
\qquad
L([a]\tau g)=L(\tau g)+2a,$$

together with

$$L(\tau g)\equiv L(g)+1\pmod 2.$$

Consequently, for fixed  $j$ , the two  $N_1$ -branches together contain at most one orbit point with largest index  $j$ : their index progressions have opposite parity, and within each progression  $a$  is uniquely determined.
On  $N_1$ ,  $\sigma$  adds  $1$  to every Zeckendorf index, hence

$$L(\sigma n)=L(n)+1.$$

Thus the  $\sigma$ -branches contribute at most one additional point to layer  $j$ . Freeness of the  $H$ -action prevents collisions between different generators. Therefore each generating orbit contributes at most two points, giving

$$N_j(k)\le 2|G(k)|=2\Psi(k).$$

The cases  $k=1,2$  may then be treated separately as in the manuscript. This is the argument that the current prose gestures toward, but every displayed index identity must actually be proved.
The accompanying Dirichlet-series step should also be written explicitly. For  $s>2$ , put

$$A(s):=\sum_{n\ge2}\frac{\varphi(n)}{n^s}
     =\frac{\zeta(s-1)}{\zeta(s)}-1.$$

The free-monoid decomposition and Tonelli give

$$1+\sum_{k\ge2}\frac{\Psi(k)}{k^s}
   =\sum_{r\ge0}A(s)^r.$$

Hence convergence is equivalent to  $A(s)<1$ , i.e.

$$\frac{\zeta(s-1)}{\zeta(s)}<2.$$

This proves the precise abscissa claim without appealing circularly to Equation (4.2) “in its half-plane of convergence.” Weinstein’s published statement supplies eventual stabilization, not the manuscript’s required uniform inequality. Weinstein, Theorem 5.1 and proof
I-02: prove nonempty fibers
Add the following lemma before negative tilts.

Lemma. Every integer  $0\le r<F_{m+2}$  is a subset sum of  $F_1,\ldots,F_m$ .

Proof by induction. For  $m=1$ , the sums are  $0,1$ . If the claim holds for  $m-1$ , then the subset sums using  $F_1,\ldots,F_m$  contain

$$[0,F_{m+1}-1]
\quad\text{and}\quad
F_m+[0,F_{m+1}-1]
   =[F_m,F_{m+2}-1].$$

These intervals overlap because  $F_m\le F_{m+1}$ , so their union is

$$[0,F_{m+2}-1].$$

Therefore  $\widetilde d_m(r)\ge1$  for every residue. Theorem 3.6 then gives  $d_m(x)\ge1$  for every  $x\in X_m$ , making  $d_m(x)^t$  well-defined for every real  $t$ .
I-03: correct the threshold claim
Replace “freezing threshold” by a statement such as:

Let  $\sigma_D$  be determined by

$$\zeta(\sigma_D-1)/\zeta(\sigma_D)=2.$$

We prove the certified frozen region

$$P(t)=0\qquad(t\le-\sigma_D).$$

We do not determine whether this is the maximal zero-pressure interval.

The terms “freezing point,” “positive-pressure phase,” and “above the freezing threshold” should likewise be made conditional. Establishing that  $-\sigma_D$  is the exact threshold would require a lower bound proving  $P(t)>0$  for every  $t>-\sigma_D$ , which is absent.
I-04: repair Proposition 4.6
For each  $m$ ,

$$p_m(t):=\frac1m\log\sum_x d_m(x)^t$$

is convex by Hölder’s inequality. Under the representation hypothesis,  $p_m(t)\to P(t)=\log r(t)$  for every real  $t$ . Hence  $P$  is finite and convex on  $\mathbb R$ , therefore continuous; consequently  $r(t)=e^{P(t)}$  is continuous.
Fix  $t_0$ . Choose a contour  $\Gamma$  enclosing  $r(t_0)$  and no other point of  $\sigma(L_{t_0})$ . For complex  $z$  near  $t_0$ , define the Riesz projection

$$\Pi(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_\Gamma
       (\lambda I-L_z)^{-1}\,d\lambda .$$

It is analytic and has rank one. It therefore determines a unique analytic eigenvalue branch  $\mu(z)$  inside  $\Gamma$ . Continuity of  $r(t)$ , together with the local spectral gap, implies

$$r(t)=\mu(t)$$

for real  $t$  sufficiently close to  $t_0$ . Thus  $r$ , and hence  $\log r$ , is locally real analytic everywhere. The identity theorem then gives the desired contradiction.
Alternatively, add continuity of  $r(t)$  or uniqueness of the distinguished positive eigenvalue directly to the proposition’s hypotheses.
I-05: clarify novelty and prior art
The introduction should explicitly say:


Theorems 3.1–3.2 are standard Fourier reformulations.


Theorem 4.2 is an exact convention-and-interval transfer of published extremal results.


Theorems 5.4 and 6.2 transfer or reprove Sanna’s results.


The substantive new claims are the explicit affine permutation in Theorem 3.6, the interval fiber identity in Theorem 4.1, the uniform early-layer strengthening and freezing theorem in §4.2, and the direct finite-window recurrence in Theorem 5.9.


That separation would make the paper’s contribution both more credible and easier to evaluate.

# Literature Check: Real-Tilt Pressure and Large Deviations

Date of search: 2026-08-02 (Asia/Singapore).

## Search protocol

The arXiv Atom API (`https://export.arxiv.org/api/query`) was queried directly.
Bibliographic metadata and DOI records were checked against arXiv metadata,
OpenAlex, publisher DOI records, and the manuscript's existing references. A
Crossref REST query was also attempted; the endpoint returned HTTP 429 during
this search, so no claim below depends on an unavailable Crossref response.

The principal arXiv queries and results were as follows.

1. `all:"Fibonacci partition"` returned 12 records. The directly relevant
   records were Kempton, *The Dynamics of the Fibonacci Partition Function*,
   arXiv:2311.06006; Sanna, *A note on the power sums of the number of
   Fibonacci partitions*, arXiv:2309.12724; Chow--Jones, *On the variance of
   the Fibonacci partition function*, arXiv:2308.15415; Chow--Slattery, *On
   Fibonacci partitions*, arXiv:2009.08222; and Weinstein, *Notes on Fibonacci
   Partitions*, arXiv:math/0307150.
2. `all:"Fibonacci partition" AND (all:pressure OR all:"large deviation")`
   returned 0 records.
3. `all:"Fibonacci partition function" AND (all:moment OR
   all:thermodynamic)` returned 0 records.
4. `all:"Thermodynamic formalism for countable Markov shifts"` returned four
   later records using Sarig's formalism, including Iommi--Yayama,
   arXiv:1106.0720, DOI 10.1088/0951-7715/25/1/165. The original Sarig papers
   are publisher-indexed rather than arXiv-indexed.
5. `au:Sarig AND all:"phase transitions"` returned 0 records. The original
   published phase-transition paper was separately verified by DOI.
6. `all:"Gartner-Ellis" AND all:"large deviations"` returned seven later
   applications/generalizations; the original Gartner and Ellis articles were
   separately verified by DOI.
7. `all:"Ruelle-Perron-Frobenius" AND all:analytic` returned Giulietti et al.,
   *The calculus of thermodynamical formalism*, arXiv:1508.01297, as a modern
   compact-state comparator.

These searches do not constitute an exhaustive mathematical-novelty proof.
They establish only that no arXiv record located by the stated direct queries
claims the present finite-window real-tilt pressure theorem or LDP.

## Exact references and use

- D. Ruelle, *Thermodynamic Formalism*, 2nd ed., Cambridge University Press,
  2004, DOI 10.1017/CBO9780511617546. This is cited for the classical compact,
  spectral-gap Ruelle--Perron--Frobenius paradigm. Its hypotheses are not
  asserted for the present multiplicity cocycle.
- O. M. Sarig, "Thermodynamic formalism for countable Markov shifts,"
  *Ergodic Theory Dynam. Systems* 19 (1999), 1565--1593,
  DOI 10.1017/S0143385799146820. This is cited for the countable-state
  recurrence/RPF framework.
- O. M. Sarig, "Phase transitions for countable Markov shifts,"
  *Comm. Math. Phys.* 217 (2001), 555--577,
  DOI 10.1007/s002200100367. This is cited to distinguish countable-state
  phase-transition behavior from globally analytic compact-state pressure.
- J. Gartner, "On large deviations from the invariant measure,"
  *Theory Probab. Appl.* 22 (1977), 24--39, DOI 10.1137/1122003; and
  R. S. Ellis, "Large deviations for a general class of random vectors,"
  *Ann. Probab.* 12 (1984), 1--12, DOI 10.1214/aop/1176993370. These are the
  original large-deviation references behind the Gartner--Ellis theorem.
- A. Dembo and O. Zeitouni, *Large Deviations Techniques and Applications*,
  2nd ed., Springer, 2010, DOI 10.1007/978-3-642-03311-7, Theorem 2.3.6. This
  is the precise modern theorem invoked for the conditional full LDP.
- C. Sanna, "A note on the power sums of the number of Fibonacci partitions,"
  *Discrete Analysis* 2025:2, DOI 10.19086/da.137601,
  arXiv:2309.12724. Sanna proves integer-power asymptotics and the integer
  zero-temperature limit; no noninteger pressure regularity is claimed there.
- F. V. Weinstein, "Notes on Fibonacci partitions," *Experimental
  Mathematics* 25 (2016), 482--499,
  DOI 10.1080/10586458.2015.1118416, arXiv:math/0307150. The manuscript uses
  Weinstein's Theorems 3.8, 5.1, and 10.3 for the generating-orbit level
  decomposition and the exact Dirichlet series
  `1 + sum Psi(k) k^{-s} = (2 - zeta(s-1)/zeta(s))^{-1}`. The published proof
  is cited, not reproduced.
- T. Kempton, "The Dynamics of the Fibonacci Partition Function,"
  arXiv:2311.06006. This provides a matrix-cocycle/irrational-rotation
  description of local Fibonacci-partition dynamics, but does not establish
  the finite-window real-moment pressure or its large deviations.

## Novelty conclusion and obstruction

The requested theorem, namely a finite all-real pressure that is real analytic
and strictly convex on the whole real line, is false. Weinstein's published
level-set orbit decomposition implies an exact frozen phase. If

`zeta(sigma_0 - 1) / zeta(sigma_0) = 2`, with
`sigma_0 = 2.478750785733960...`, then the manuscript proves

`P(t) = 0` for every `t <= -sigma_0`.

Since `P(0) = log(phi)`, analytic continuation from the frozen open half-line
would give a contradiction. This also excludes strict convexity on all of the
real line. The new, fully proved contribution is the transfer of Weinstein's
level-orbit Dirichlet structure through the paper's exact two-layer fiber
identity to obtain this finite-window freezing theorem, including the critical
endpoint. No located reference states this finite-window consequence.

The residual open interface is existence for every
`t in (-sigma_0, infinity)`, differentiability at `-sigma_0`, and analytic
positive curvature within the open positive-pressure phase. Consequently the
full LDP remains conditional on this corrected residual hypothesis. The paper
does not claim that general Ruelle or Sarig theory verifies the required
operator hypotheses for this cocycle.

\title[Finite-window Zeckendorf fibers]{%
  Finite-Window Zeckendorf Fibers:\\
  Affine Partition Correspondence and Negative-Temperature Freezing}

\author{Haobo Ma}
\address{AELF PTE LTD., \#14-02, Marina Bay Financial Centre Tower 1,
8 Marina Blvd, Singapore 018981, Singapore}
\email{auric@aelf.io}

\author{Wenlin Zhang}
\address{National University of Singapore, Singapore}
\email{e1327962@u.nus.edu}
\hypersetup{
  pdftitle={Finite-Window Zeckendorf Fibers: Affine Partition Correspondence and Negative-Temperature Freezing},
  pdfauthor={Haobo Ma and Wenlin Zhang},
  pdfsubject={Finite-window fibers of Fibonacci subset sums},
  pdfkeywords={Zeckendorf representation, Fibonacci partition function, extremal fibers, power sums, pressure, negative-temperature freezing}

\date{}
\subjclass[2020]{11B39, 05A17, 60F10}
\keywords{Zeckendorf representation, Fibonacci partition function, extremal

\begin{document}

\begin{abstract}
We study the fibers of a finite-window map that sends a binary Fibonacci sum
to the legal word representing its residue modulo the next Fibonacci number.
An explicit affine permutation identifies the fiber multiplicities with the
coefficients of a finite Fibonacci subset-sum polynomial and, pointwise, with
a Fibonacci-lag difference of the classical partition function.  A second
identity identifies the complete fiber list with values of that partition
function on two consecutive intervals.  The known extremal classification on
these intervals then gives
\(D_{2k}=F_{k+2}\), \(D_{2k+1}=2F_{k+1}\), and
\(D_m=D_{m-2}+D_{m-4}\) for \(m\ge6\), together with all maximizing
residues.  For integer \(q\ge1\), the fiber power sums have the same
exponential constants as the partition power sums studied by Sanna; in the
quadratic case we give a direct cubic recurrence.  For real negative tilts we
prove a Dirichlet-series freezing bound.  If \(\sigma_0>2\) is determined by
\(\zeta(\sigma_0-1)/\zeta(\sigma_0)=2\), then
\(\lim_{m\to\infty}m^{-1}\log\sum_x d_m(x)^t=0\) for every
\(t\le-\sigma_0\).  The endpoint proof uses an explicit transfer of
Weinstein's orbit formulas, including the exceptional levels and early
layers.  We do not determine whether this is the maximal zero-pressure
interval.  The proved frozen half-line rules out a globally analytic
compact-operator
representation under precise spectral hypotheses.
\end{abstract}

\section{Finite-window fibers}\label{sec:preliminaries}

Throughout, the Fibonacci numbers are indexed by
\[
  F_0=0,
  \qquad
  F_1=F_2=1,
  \qquad
  F_{n+2}=F_{n+1}+F_n
  \qquad (n\ge 0).
\]

\subsection{Stable words and the residue model}

\begin{definition}[Stable words and visible values]
\label{def:stable-words}
For \(m\ge 0\), let
\[
  \Omega_m:=\{0,1\}^m,
  \qquad
  X_m:=\{x\in\Omega_m:\ x_ix_{i+1}=0\text{ for }1\le i<m\}.
\]
For \(x\in X_m\), define
\[
  V_m(x):=\sum_{j=1}^m x_jF_{j+1}.
\]
For \(\omega=\omega_1\cdots \omega_m\in\Omega_m\), define
\[
  N_m(\omega):=\sum_{j=1}^m \omega_jF_{j+1}.
\]
Thus \(\Omega_0=X_0=\{\varepsilon\}\), where \(\varepsilon\) is the empty word,
and \(V_0(\varepsilon)=N_0(\varepsilon)=0\).
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Visible values are complete]
\label{prop:Vm-bijection}
For every \(m\ge 0\), the map
\[
  V_m\colon X_m\longrightarrow \{0,1,\dots,F_{m+2}-1\}
\]
is a bijection.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The zero word maps to \(0\), so it remains to treat
\(n\in\{1,\dots,F_{m+2}-1\}\).  By Zeckendorf's theorem
\cite{Zeckendorf1972}, \(n\) has a unique
expansion as a sum of nonconsecutive Fibonacci numbers.  Because
\(n<F_{m+2}\), that expansion cannot use the summand \(F_{m+2}\) or any larger
Fibonacci number; therefore it uses only the weights \(F_2,\dots,F_{m+1}\),
and hence determines a unique word \(x\in X_m\) with \(V_m(x)=n\).
Conversely every \(x\in X_m\) is a stable expansion in those same weights, so
\(V_m(x)\in\{0,\dots,F_{m+2}-1\}\).  Thus \(V_m\) is bijective.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Finite-window fold]
\label{def:fold-map}
For \(m\ge 0\), define
\[
  \Fold_m(\omega):=
  V_m^{-1}\bigl(N_m(\omega)\bmod F_{m+2}\bigr),
  \qquad \omega\in\Omega_m.
\]
For \(x\in X_m\), define the fiber multiplicity
\[
  d_m(x):=\card{\Fold_m^{-1}(x)}.
\]
In particular, \(\Fold_0(\varepsilon)=\varepsilon\) and
\(d_0(\varepsilon)=1\).
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Single overflow]
\label{prop:single-overflow}
For every \(m\ge0\) and every \(\omega\in\Omega_m\),
\[
  N_m(\omega)\le \sum_{j=1}^m F_{j+1}=F_{m+3}-2<2F_{m+2}.
\]
Hence there exist unique integers \(b\in\{0,1\}\) and \(r\in[0,F_{m+2})\) such
that
\[
  N_m(\omega)=bF_{m+2}+r,
\]
and
\[
  \Fold_m(\omega)=V_m^{-1}(r).
\]
Equivalently, there is a unique overflow bit \(b_m(\omega)\in\{0,1\}\) such
that
\[
  N_m(\omega)=V_m(\Fold_m(\omega))+b_m(\omega)F_{m+2}.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The Fibonacci identity
\(\sum_{j=1}^m F_{j+1}=F_{m+3}-2\) is standard, and
\(F_{m+3}-2<F_{m+3}-1<F_{m+3}=F_{m+2}+F_{m+1}<2F_{m+2}\).  The displayed
formula for \(\Fold_m\) is simply the definition.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Operational meaning of the fold]
\label{rem:prefix-normalization}
Because only one overflow can occur, the finite-window fold agrees with
computing the full Zeckendorf expansion of \(N_m(\omega)\) and deleting the
possible leading \(F_{m+2}\)-digit.
The residue definition is the useful one for exact fiber analysis.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Residue characterization of fibers]
\label{cor:residue-characterization}
For \(x\in X_m\), write \(r_x:=V_m(x)\).  Then
\[
  \Fold_m(\omega)=x
  \iff
  N_m(\omega)\equiv r_x \pmod{F_{m+2}}.
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
By definition,
\(\Fold_m(\omega)=V_m^{-1}(N_m(\omega)\bmod F_{m+2})\), and \(V_m\) is
bijective by \Cref{prop:Vm-bijection}.
\end{proof}

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\caption{Notation used throughout the paper.  The letter \(q\) is reserved
for a nonnegative integer moment order, whereas \(t\) denotes a real tilt.}
\label{tab:notation}
\begin{tabular}{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule
Symbol & Meaning \\
\midrule
\(\Omega_m\), \(X_m\) & raw binary words and legal visible words \\
\(\Fold_m\) & residue-defined finite-window fold \\
\(d_m(x)\), \(d_m^\#(r)\) & fiber multiplicity, indexed by a word or residue \\
\(\widetilde d_m(n)\) & coefficient of \(\prod_{j=1}^m(1+z^{F_j})\) \\
\(R(n)\), \(R^\dagger(n)\) & ordinary and indexed Fibonacci partition counts \\
\(S_q(m)\), \(S_t(m)\) & integer-order moment and real-tilt sum \\
\(T_q^\dagger(N)\) & indexed partition power sum below \(N\) \\
\(D_m\) & largest fiber multiplicity \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

 succeeded in 188ms:
\section{Affine transfer and partition differences}\label{sec:residue-affine}

\subsection{Fourier formulas for the fibers}

\begin{theorem}[Residue fibers]
\label{thm:residue-fibers}
For every \(x\in X_m\), with \(r_x:=V_m(x)\),
\[
  d_m(x)
  =
  \card{\Bigl\{\omega\in\Omega_m:
  \sum_{j=1}^m\omega_jF_{j+1}\equiv r_x \pmod{F_{m+2}}\Bigr\}}.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
This is exactly \Cref{cor:residue-characterization}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Fourier inversion]
\label{thm:fourier-inversion}
Let \(\zeta=e^{2\pi i/F_{m+2}}\).  Then for every \(x\in X_m\),
\[
  d_m(x)=\frac{1}{F_{m+2}}
  \sum_{a=0}^{F_{m+2}-1}
  \zeta^{-ar_x}\prod_{j=1}^m(1+\zeta^{aF_{j+1}}).
\]
Consequently, if
\[
  \Sigma_m:=\sum_{x\in X_m}d_m(x)^2,
\]
then
\[
  \Sigma_m=
  \frac{1}{F_{m+2}}
  \sum_{a=0}^{F_{m+2}-1}
  \left|
    \prod_{j=1}^m(1+\zeta^{aF_{j+1}})
  \right|^2.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
In the finite cyclic group \(\ZZ/F_{m+2}\ZZ\),
\[
  \mathbf{1}_{n\equiv r_x}
  =
  \frac{1}{F_{m+2}}
  \sum_{a=0}^{F_{m+2}-1}\zeta^{a(n-r_x)}.
\]
Therefore
\[
  d_m(x)
  =
  \sum_{\omega\in\Omega_m}\mathbf{1}_{N_m(\omega)\equiv r_x}
  =
  \frac{1}{F_{m+2}}
  \sum_{a=0}^{F_{m+2}-1}
  \zeta^{-ar_x}
  \sum_{\omega\in\Omega_m}\zeta^{aN_m(\omega)}.
\]
Since \(N_m(\omega)=\sum_{j=1}^m \omega_jF_{j+1}\),
\[
  \sum_{\omega\in\Omega_m}\zeta^{aN_m(\omega)}
  =
  \prod_{j=1}^m\sum_{\omega_j\in\{0,1\}}\zeta^{a\omega_jF_{j+1}}
  =
  \prod_{j=1}^m(1+\zeta^{aF_{j+1}}),
\]
which proves the first formula.

For the second formula, use that \(d_m(x)\in\RR\), so
\[
  \Sigma_m=\sum_{x\in X_m}|d_m(x)|^2 .
\]
Multiply the first identity by its complex conjugate and sum over \(x\in X_m\):
\[
  \Sigma_m
  =
  \frac{1}{F_{m+2}^2}
  \sum_{a,b=0}^{F_{m+2}-1}
  \left(
    \prod_{j=1}^m(1+\zeta^{aF_{j+1}})
  \right)
  \overline{
    \left(
      \prod_{j=1}^m(1+\zeta^{bF_{j+1}})
    \right)
  }
  \sum_{x\in X_m}\zeta^{(b-a)r_x}.
\]
Because \(V_m\) is a bijection onto \(\{0,\dots,F_{m+2}-1\}\), the residues
\(\{r_x:x\in X_m\}\) are exactly the full residue system.  Hence the character
orthogonality relation
\[
  \sum_{x\in X_m}\zeta^{(b-a)r_x}
  =
  \sum_{r=0}^{F_{m+2}-1}\zeta^{(b-a)r}
  =
  \begin{cases}
    F_{m+2}, & a=b,\\
    0, & a\neq b
  \end{cases}
\]
eliminates the cross terms and gives
\[
  \Sigma_m
  =
  \frac{1}{F_{m+2}}
  \sum_{a=0}^{F_{m+2}-1}
  \left|
    \prod_{j=1}^m(1+\zeta^{aF_{j+1}})
  \right|^2,
\]
as claimed.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Affine transfer to Fibonacci subset sums}

\begin{definition}[Shifted subset-sum spectrum]
\label{def:shifted-spectrum}
Define
\[
  \widetilde N_m(\omega):=\sum_{j=1}^m \omega_jF_j
  \qquad (\omega\in\Omega_m),
\]
and
\[
  \widetilde d_m(r):=
  \card{\{\omega\in\Omega_m:\ \widetilde N_m(\omega)=r\}},
  \qquad 0\le r<F_{m+2}.
\]
Because \(\sum_{j=1}^m F_j=F_{m+2}-1\), no reduction modulo \(F_{m+2}\) is
needed in this definition.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[The affine residue permutation]
\label{def:affine-map}
Define \(\tau_m\colon \Omega_m\to\Omega_m\) by
\[
  (\tau_m(\omega))_k=
  \begin{cases}
    \omega_{m+1-k}, & k\equiv m \pmod 2,\\[4pt]
    1-\omega_{m+1-k}, & k\not\equiv m \pmod 2,
  \end{cases}
  \qquad 1\le k\le m.
\]
Define
\[
  C_m:=\sum_{\substack{1\le k\le m\\ k\not\equiv m \,(\mathrm{mod}\,2)}}F_{k+1},
  \qquad
  \pi_m(r)\equiv F_{m+1}r+C_m \pmod{F_{m+2}}.
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Closed form for the translation term]
\label{lem:Cm-closed}
For every \(m\ge 1\),
\[
  C_m=F_{m+1}-1.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If \(m=2q\), then
\[
  C_m=F_2+F_4+\cdots+F_{2q}=F_{2q+1}-1=F_{m+1}-1.
\]
If \(m=2q+1\), then
\[
  C_m=F_3+F_5+\cdots+F_{2q+1}=F_{2q+2}-1=F_{m+1}-1.
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Affine transfer principle]
\label{thm:affine-transfer}
For every \(m\ge 1\), the map \(\pi_m\) is a permutation of
\(\ZZ/F_{m+2}\ZZ\), and
\[
  \widetilde d_m(r)=d_m^\#(\pi_m(r)),
  \qquad
  d_m^\#(s):=d_m(V_m^{-1}(s)).
\]
Equivalently, the multiset of fiber multiplicities of \(\Fold_m\) agrees with
the coefficient multiset of
\[
  \prod_{j=1}^m(1+z^{F_j})
\]
up to the explicit affine permutation \(\pi_m\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
We use d'Ocagne's identity
\[
  F_uF_{v+1}-F_{u+1}F_v=(-1)^vF_{u-v}.
\]
Taking \(u=m+1\) and \(v=j-1\) gives
\[
  F_{m+1}F_j-F_{m+2}F_{j-1}=(-1)^{j-1}F_{m+2-j},
\]
hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:docagne-window}
  F_{m+1}F_j\equiv (-1)^{j-1}F_{m+2-j}\pmod{F_{m+2}}.
\end{equation}

Now
\[
  N_m(\tau_m(\omega))
  =
  \sum_{\substack{1\le k\le m\\ k\equiv m \,(\mathrm{mod}\,2)}}
  \omega_{m+1-k}F_{k+1}
  +
  \sum_{\substack{1\le k\le m\\ k\not\equiv m \,(\mathrm{mod}\,2)}}
  (1-\omega_{m+1-k})F_{k+1}.
\]
Separate the constant term:
\[
  N_m(\tau_m(\omega))
  =
  \sum_{k=1}^m(-1)^{m-k}\omega_{m+1-k}F_{k+1}+C_m.
\]
After the change of variables \(j=m+1-k\),
\[
  N_m(\tau_m(\omega))
  =
  \sum_{j=1}^m(-1)^{j-1}\omega_jF_{m+2-j}+C_m.
\]
Applying \eqref{eq:docagne-window} term by term yields
\[
  N_m(\tau_m(\omega))
  \equiv
  F_{m+1}\sum_{j=1}^m \omega_jF_j + C_m
  =
  F_{m+1}\widetilde N_m(\omega)+C_m
  \pmod{F_{m+2}}.
\]
Thus
\begin{equation}\label{eq:tau-congruence}
  N_m(\tau_m(\omega))\equiv \pi_m(\widetilde N_m(\omega))\pmod{F_{m+2}}.
\end{equation}

Since \(\gcd(F_{m+1},F_{m+2})=1\), multiplication by \(F_{m+1}\) is invertible
modulo \(F_{m+2}\), so \(\pi_m\) is a permutation.  Because \(\tau_m\) is also
a bijection of \(\Omega_m\), for every residue \(r\),
\begin{align*}
  \widetilde d_m(r)
  &=
  \card{\{\omega\in\Omega_m:\ \widetilde N_m(\omega)=r\}} \\
  &=
  \card{
    \left\{
      \omega\in\Omega_m:
      N_m(\tau_m(\omega))\equiv \pi_m(r)\pmod{F_{m+2}}
    \right\}
  }.
\end{align*}
By \Cref{thm:residue-fibers}, the last quantity is precisely
\(d_m^\#(\pi_m(r))\).
\end{proof}

\begin{intervalcompletenesslemma}
For every \(m\ge1\), every integer \(r\) with \(0\le r<F_{m+2}\) is a
subset sum of \(F_1,\ldots,F_m\).  Consequently,
\[
  \widetilde d_m(r)\ge1\qquad(0\le r<F_{m+2})
\]
and every fiber of \(\Fold_m\) is nonempty.
\end{intervalcompletenesslemma}

\begin{proof}
We argue by induction on \(m\).  For \(m=1\), the subset sums of \(F_1\) are
\(0\) and \(1\), which constitute the interval
\([0,F_3-1]\cap\ZZ\).  Suppose that the assertion holds for \(m-1\), where
\(m\ge2\).  The subset sums formed from \(F_1,\ldots,F_m\) include both
integer intervals
\[
  [0,F_{m+1}-1]
  \quad\text{and}\quad
  F_m+[0,F_{m+1}-1]=[F_m,F_{m+2}-1].
\]
Since \(F_m<F_{m+1}\), these intervals overlap, and their union is
\([0,F_{m+2}-1]\).  This proves the induction step and the inequality for
\(\widetilde d_m\).  The affine transfer principle, \Cref{thm:affine-transfer},
then gives
\[
  d_m^\#(\pi_m(r))=\widetilde d_m(r)\ge1.
\]
Because \(\pi_m\) permutes all residues and \(V_m\) is bijective, every fiber
is nonempty.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Coefficient-spectrum form of the fibers]
\label{cor:coefficient-spectrum}
As multisets,
\[
  \{d_m(x):x\in X_m\}
  =
  \Bigl\{
    [z^r]\prod_{j=1}^m(1+z^{F_j}) : 0\le r<F_{m+2}
  \Bigr\}.
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The coefficient \([z^r]\prod_{j=1}^m(1+z^{F_j})\) is exactly
\(\widetilde d_m(r)\).  Apply \Cref{thm:affine-transfer}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The partition-difference formula}

\begin{definition}[Fibonacci partition functions]
\label{def:partition-functions}
Let \(R(n)\) denote the number of partitions of \(n\) into distinct Fibonacci
numbers, where the two unit weights \(F_1=F_2=1\) are not distinguished, and
set \(R(n)=0\) for \(n<0\).  Following the indexed model in which the two unit
weights are distinguished, define
\[
  R^\dagger(n):=R(n)+R(n-1)
  \qquad (n\in\ZZ).
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Partition-difference formula]
\label{thm:partition-difference}
For every \(m\ge1\) and every integer \(n\) with
\(0\le n<F_{m+2}\),
\[
  d_m^\#(\pi_m(n))
  =
  R^\dagger(n)-R^\dagger(n-F_{m+1}).
\]
Equivalently,
\[
  d_m^\#(\pi_m(n))
  =
  R(n)+R(n-1)-R(n-F_{m+1})-R(n-F_{m+1}-1).
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By \Cref{thm:affine-transfer},
\[
  d_m^\#(\pi_m(n))=\widetilde d_m(n),
\]
where \(\widetilde d_m(n)\) is the coefficient of \(z^n\) in
\[
  \mathcal P_m(z):=\prod_{j=1}^m(1+z^{F_j}).
\]
Thus
\begin{equation}\label{eq:shifted-coefficient}
  d_m^\#(\pi_m(n))=[z^n]\mathcal P_m(z).
\end{equation}

The ordinary Fibonacci partition function has generating series
\[
  \sum_{n\ge 0}R(n)z^n=(1+z)\prod_{j\ge 3}(1+z^{F_j}),
\]
because the unit weight occurs only once.  Hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:indexed-generating}
  \sum_{n\ge 0}R^\dagger(n)z^n
  =
  (1+z)\sum_{n\ge 0}R(n)z^n
  =
  \prod_{j\ge 1}(1+z^{F_j}).
\end{equation}
If \(0\le n<F_{m+2}\), then the coefficient of \(z^n\) in the infinite product
\(\prod_{j\ge 1}(1+z^{F_j})\) depends only on the factors with \(j\le m+1\),
since \(F_{m+2}\) and all later Fibonacci numbers exceed \(n\).  Therefore
\[
  R^\dagger(n)
  =
  [z^n](1+z^{F_{m+1}})\mathcal P_m(z)
  =
  [z^n]\mathcal P_m(z)+[z^{n-F_{m+1}}]\mathcal P_m(z).
\]
The second coefficient is \(R^\dagger(n-F_{m+1})\), since
\(n-F_{m+1}<F_{m+1}<F_{m+2}\).  Using \eqref{eq:shifted-coefficient}, we
obtain
\[
  d_m^\#(\pi_m(n))
  =
  R^\dagger(n)-R^\dagger(n-F_{m+1}),
\]
where the second term is interpreted as \(0\) for \(n<F_{m+1}\).  Expanding
\(R^\dagger(t)=R(t)+R(t-1)\) yields the four-term formula.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[A four-digit window]
\label{ex:m4-partition-difference}
Take \(m=4\).  Then \(F_5=5\), \(F_6=8\), and
\[
  \pi_4(n)\equiv 5n+4 \pmod 8.
\]
Moreover,
\[
  \prod_{j=1}^4(1+z^{F_j})
  =
  (1+z)^2(1+z^2)(1+z^3)
  =
  \begin{aligned}[t]
  &1+2z+2z^2+3z^3\\
  &\quad{}+3z^4+2z^5+2z^6+z^7,
  \end{aligned}
\]
so the difference spectrum is
\[
  \bigl(R^\dagger(n)-R^\dagger(n-5)\bigr)_{n=0}^{7}
  =
  (1,2,2,3,3,2,2,1).
\]
Applying \(\pi_4\) sends \(0,1,\dots,7\) to \(4,1,6,3,0,5,2,7\), and hence
\[
  \bigl(d_4^\#(r)\bigr)_{r=0}^{7}
  =
  (3,2,2,3,1,2,2,1).
\]
Thus the affine permutation simply reindexes the coefficient list of
\(\prod_{j=1}^4(1+z^{F_j})\) into the finite-window fiber spectrum.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}[Single-fiber formulas from classical partition theory]
\label{rem:single-fiber-literature}
Theorem~\ref{thm:partition-difference} identifies each fiber multiplicity with
a Fibonacci-lag difference of the classical partition function.  Hence the
exact Zeckendorf formulas of Chow and Slattery \cite{ChowSlattery2021} apply
directly to \(d_m^\#(\pi_m(n))\).
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Complement symmetry]
\label{prop:complement-symmetry}
Let
\[
  B_m:=\sum_{j=1}^mF_{j+1}=F_{m+3}-2.
\]
Then, as a function on \(\ZZ/F_{m+2}\ZZ\),
\[
  d_m^\#(r)=d_m^\#(B_m-r).
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let \(\iota(\omega)_j:=1-\omega_j\).  Then \(\iota\) is a bijection of
\(\Omega_m\), and
\[
  N_m(\iota(\omega))
  =
  \sum_{j=1}^m (1-\omega_j)F_{j+1}
  =
  B_m-N_m(\omega).
\]
Hence
\[
  N_m(\omega)\equiv r \pmod{F_{m+2}}
  \iff
  N_m(\iota(\omega))\equiv B_m-r \pmod{F_{m+2}}.
\]
Apply \Cref{thm:residue-fibers}.
\end{proof}
